- Merton, a Trappist monk, approaches the experience of nothingness from a distinctly religious perspective, but his ideas converge with Heidegger's, suggesting that their viewpoints might not be as disparate as they initially appear, with both critiquing the constant immersion in worldly affairs as a way of avoiding the fundamental encounter with nothingness.
- The potential shared language for understanding nihilistic human experience emerges from the recognition of the primacy of the experience of nothingness itself, regardless of theistic or atheistic interpretations, with both Heidegger and Merton pointing to this fundamental encounter with the void or inner emptiness as a crucial aspect of the human condition.
- A shared language could describe the state of distraction and the eventual breakdown of its capacity to shield one from the underlying nothingness, with both Heidegger's relentless questioning and Merton's reverence for mystery suggesting a posture of open inquiry in the face of the unknown.
- The development of a shared language is proposed to authentically engage with the nihilistic experience, emphasizing a questioning stance and avoiding premature closure through dogma or simplistic answers, as seen in the works of thinkers like Heidegger and Merton.
- This shared language would need to be symbolic rather than literal, as finite human language struggles to grasp the Infinite or the Wholly-Other, and would require a self-critical and open approach to reinterpretation, as evident in Heidegger's evocative terminology and Merton's theological language.

Transcendence, Nihiltheism, and the Challenges of Language

- The concept of "[[Transcendence (philosophy) | Transcendence]]" is introduced as a neutral term to describe a feature of human experience that points beyond the immediate worldly realm, and a shared language could explore this yearning for transcendence without being confined to specific religious or naturalist worldviews.
- The proposed term "Nihiltheism" is suggested as a grounding word for this genre of human experience, aiming to describe the experience where Transcendence is revealed through [[Nihilism | nihilism]] without relying on myth, tradition, or purely naturalistic explanations.
- The development of such a shared language is acknowledged to be challenging due to the non-transferable nature of the experience of nihilism, requiring a nuanced and self-critical approach to language development, as highlighted by the difficulties in articulating profound experiences through finite human language.

The Non-Transferable Nature of Nihilistic Experience

- The concept of understanding nihilism requires a direct experience, rather than just intellectual understanding, and this experience is difficult to capture linguistically, as noted by [[Thérèse of Lisieux | Therese of Lisieux]], who suggests that one must pass through darkness to understand its blackness.
- A proposed language for understanding nihilistic human experience is envisioned as tentative, doubtful, and questioning, serving as a "map" through the nothingness of the world, potentially leading towards [[Transcendence (philosophy) | transcendence]], but it should not be taken as complete or literal.
- The language is likely to be symbolic, self-critical, and focused on the direct experience of confronting ultimate [[Śūnyatā | emptiness]] and the yearning for transcendence, potentially grounded in Heidegger's phenomenological vocabulary and acknowledging the mystical dimensions highlighted by Merton.

Heidegger's Influence on the Language of Nihilism

- Heidegger's influence is presented as a crucial foundation for understanding and articulating the experience of [[Nihilism | nihilism]], particularly in the development of a potential "language of Nihilism", with his comprehensive analysis of the human condition in [[Being and Time]] offering a framework and vocabulary that can capture key insights into the nihilistic experience.
- Heidegger's concept of "Being-Towards-Death" is a central contribution, involving confronting the finitude of existence and the inherent possibility of death at any moment, accompanied by anxiety in the face of the "nothing" of the world itself, and this experience is a key aspect of encountering nihilism.
- The "already-made" language of Heidegger provides essential tools for interpreting the Nihilistic experience, with terms like "anxiety", "moments of vision", "everydayness", "the call of conscience", "authenticity", "uncanniness", and "resoluteness" capturing key aspects of the human condition relevant to nihilism, and allowing for an interpretation of nihilism that is grounded in the naturalistic aspect of human existence while pointing towards something beyond a purely naturalistic understanding.
- The concept of "Nihiltheism" represents an attempt to articulate a middle ground, a language that can speak to the profound depths of nihilism while remaining open to the possibility of something beyond the purely naturalistic, and it is suggested that further exploration of this concept, as well as the contributions of other thinkers such as Nietzsche or Cioran, could provide additional insights into the discussion.

Critiques of Heidegger and the Concept of the 'They'

- The section discusses the influence of Martin Heidegger's philosophy on the development of a "language of [[Nihilism]]", with some critics, such as Tønnessen and Heisman, expressing concerns that his use of "exotic language" may be a way to avoid confronting the reality of human existence.
- Heidegger's ideas are compared to those of [[Thomas Merton]], a Trappist monk, who describes penetrating inner [[Śūnyatā | emptiness]] with "awe and reverence", raising questions about the distinction between their perspectives and whether the use of the word "God" is enough to fundamentally distinguish them.
- The section also explores Heidegger's critique of traditional philosophy and science, warning against a positivistic and scientism-style of thinking, and arguing that science has become a technical and practical matter that lacks the potential for a true "awakening of the spirit".
- The concept of the "They" (das Man) from Heidegger's philosophy is highlighted, noting that in everydayness, individuals tend to fall into the inauthenticity of the "They", becoming absorbed in public norms and "idle talk", and that this can lead to a "downward plunge" that is often misinterpreted as "ascending".
- Heidegger's framework is presented as a significant resource for understanding how individuals avoid confronting the fundamental questions of existence that nihilism brings to the fore, and his work provides concepts for interpreting the human condition in a way that transcends both purely naturalistic and dogmatic religious perspectives.
- The section concludes by suggesting that Heidegger's philosophy, with its unique vocabulary and focus on fundamental questions of Being, particularly "Being-Towards-Death" and the experience of anxiety, can be a starting point for articulating the profound and often inexpressible experience of nihilistic nothingness, and that this can potentially lead to an understanding and experience of [[Transcendence (philosophy) | Transcendence]] through the lens of nihilism.

Nihilism as a Path to Transcendence

- The concept of [[Nihilism | nihilism]] is redefined as a potential stepping stone towards encountering something beyond the "Nothing of the world", referred to as "the Other" or a form of Transcendence, rather than a purely destructive conclusion.
- The journey towards Transcendence through nihilism begins with a direct and authentic experience of the "Nothing of the world", which involves the failure of everyday distractions and the breakdown of inherent meaning, leading to a confrontation with a reality devoid of pre-established value, as noted by James and Nietzsche.
- This confrontation with nihilistic nothingness can act as a "clearing of the path" by recognizing the futility of worldly endeavors, allowing one to renounce attachments to the transient and illusory aspects of existence, and prepare for a possible encounter with Transcendence, as stated by Huxley.
- The experience of nihilism, rather than being purely negative, might contain the potential for something more profound, with the realization that "everything is nothing" being the initial revelation of mysticism, and the step from nothing to God being a small one, as suggested by Cioran.
- The concept of "the Other" is central to understanding [[Transcendence (philosophy) | Transcendence]] through nihilism, representing something wholly different from the mundane, naturalistic world, and not necessarily defined by traditional religious frameworks, with the confrontation with the Nothing of the world creating a "suspension" or a "pushing" to encounter this Other.
- Heidegger's analysis of "Being-Towards-Death", with its emphasis on anxiety in the face of nothingness, is seen as a potential framework for understanding this encounter, where anxiety can lead to a more authentic mode of being, and potentially, an initial step towards recognizing something beyond the immediate, worldly existence.
- The concept of "Nihiltheism" is proposed as a way to describe the experience of Transcendence through [[Nihilism | nihilism]], which does not rely on traditional myth, tradition, or naturalistic explanations, and instead uses a symbolic and self-critical language that is open to reinterpretation.
- This approach contrasts with traditional religious and naturalistic worldviews, which often provide pre-packaged answers that prevent an authentic encounter with the underlying nothingness, or fail to recognize the deeper implications of the nihilistic experience.
- The idea of Nihiltheism suggests that experiencing the "empty structure of Naturalism" can become a "ladder into Transcendence," and that psychedelic experiences can be an "Augmented" form of nihilism, leading to a more direct encounter with "Mystical Nothingness" and "the Other".
- The nature of this transcendent experience is often described as ineﬀable, and language struggles to capture its essence, highlighting the need for a symbolic rather than literal approach, and the "unending questioning of 'why?'" that arises from the experience of nihilism can itself open up the possibility of something not wholly natural.
- The exploration of [[Transcendence (philosophy) | Transcendence]] through nihilism acknowledges the inherent difficulties and uncertainties, and does not offer a simple path or a guaranteed outcome, but instead suggests that the courageous and authentic confrontation with the deepest levels of meaninglessness might paradoxically open up the possibility of experiencing or understanding something that transcends the limitations of our ordinary, meaning-seeking consciousness.

Dread, Nihilating Behavior, and the Divided Self

- The concept of "the Other" is related to Transcendence through [[Nihilism | nihilism]], and is described as a potential encounter that involves a dissolution of the ego and a feeling of unity, and the sources establish that a fundamental dread or anxiety related to the "no-thing" is a permanent, though often suppressed, aspect of human existence.
- The concept of dread reveals the untenability of "being as a whole," which is characterized by "nihilating behavior" that points to the underlying awareness of the "no-thing" disclosed by dread, as human existence is marked by this inherent awareness.
- The original dread is often suppressed in everyday existence through various means such as busyness and distractions, but it can resurface through the "why-question," which can lead to a moment of confronting the underlying lack of inherent meaning.
- The philosopher Buber describes anxiety as the "stirring of the creature between the realms of plantlike security and spiritual risk," which brings the concept of "no-thing" to the forefront, and this idea is related to the naturalistic interpretation of nihilism, which is the "fuller" or "concrete" expression of a suspicion of meaninglessness.
- The naturalistic interpretation of nihilism is also described as the "Nothing of the world," and profound thinkers often discuss diminished, worldly versions of this nihilism, highlighting the complexity of the concept.
- The sources emphasize a fundamental split within human consciousness, which is divided between the naturalistic, finite concerns of the world and a transcendent potential or awareness, resulting in a divided self that is pulled between these two opposing forces, as described by philosophers such as Buber.

Transfiguration, Mystical Experiences, and the Absurd

- The section discusses various philosophical and spiritual concepts, including the idea that routine existence is a form of "sheer madness" that needs to be overcome to achieve transfiguration, as highlighted by Cioran, and that psychedelics, such as [[Psilocybin | psilocybin]], can be a tool for inducing mystical experiences characterized by ego eradication and a feeling of unity with a "bigger something" or the "Other".
- The text also explores the concepts of renunciation and humility, as well as the Buddhist perspective on [[Śūnyatā | emptiness]], which is described as a "zero full of infinite possibilities" that transcends dualities and the limitations of the relative world, and is echoed across various mystical and religious traditions, including the works of Pseudo-Dionysius, Eckhart, and Molinos.
- The experience of "Nothingness" is a central theme, which can be equated with the ground of all things yet is itself "nothing" in a super-essential sense, and is described as a transcendent experience that involves encountering the abyss of the unknown, as discussed by Nietzsche and others.
- The section also touches on the idea that music, particularly abstract music like Bach's organ music, can be a guide for navigating the confrontation with the "Other" and the death of the "I", and that the absurd valuation of conventional values, as described by Nietzsche, can lead to a form of [[Nihilism | nihilism]] where even one's own happiness, reason, and virtues become repulsive.
- Additionally, the text discusses the concept of the multiverse and the triviality of choice, where the hypothetical existence of a multiverse can lead to a form of nihilism where all choices become trivial due to the infinite number of parallel selves making all possible decisions, and the questioning of worldly meaning in the face of the vastness of the universe and the inevitability of death and cosmic insignificance.
- The section also explores the distinction between theory and realization, as emphasized by [[Swami Vivekananda | Vivekananda]], who argues that intellectual understanding of spiritual or philosophical concepts is insufficient, and that true knowledge requires "realization", a direct experience, and the concept of the "tragedy of the ego", which is described as a tragic blunder that dissolves with the understanding that "nobody is ever born and nobody ever dies", as borrowed from Zen Buddhism.
- Furthermore, the text includes a first-hand account of an experience on August 27th involving a dissolution of the universe, out-of-body sensations, and a profound encounter with something "Other", mediated by Bach's music, which defeats the author's prior naturalistic worldview, and introduces the idea of "Nihiltheism", which suggests that the ultimate reality might be non-moral, and that uniting with this reality does not necessarily lead to moral behavior.
- Finally, the section expresses skepticism towards organized religion and its pragmatic concerns, advocating for a more direct, experiential approach to the Transcendent, as echoed by various thinkers, including Nietzsche, who links the ascetic ideal to humanity's "horror vacui", the need for a goal, even if it is nothingness itself, rather than no will at all.

Philosophical Perspectives on Transcendence and the Human Condition

- The concept of "[[Transcendence (philosophy) | Transcendence]]" is proposed as a neutral alternative to the term "religious", and various philosophers, including [[Blaise Pascal | Pascal]], Nietzsche, and Heidegger, discuss the human condition, [[Nihilism | nihilism]], and the importance of direct experience in understanding the transcendent.
- The philosophers mentioned, such as Nietzsche, Heidegger, and [[Swami Vivekananda | Vivekananda]], emphasize the need to acknowledge and confront the "nothingness" or the void that exists in human existence, and that this experience is fundamental to relating to being and understanding the human condition.
- The importance of personal experience and direct realization is highlighted by philosophers like Vivekananda, who suggests that one's deepest religious insights should be kept sacred and secret, and that intellectual understanding or the experiences of others are not sufficient to truly understand the transcendent.
- The critique of the "scholar" who lacks original thought and the cosmologist who studies the universe without questioning the worth of their pursuit is also discussed, with philosophers like Nietzsche and Heidegger emphasizing the need for self-reflection and confrontation with the implications of one's knowledge.
- A cast of characters, including the author, Cioran, Heidegger, Nietzsche, Vivekananda, Huxley, Buber, Tillich, and Tozer, are mentioned, each contributing their unique perspectives on the human condition, nihilism, and the transcendent, with some emphasizing the need to overcome banality, the significance of experiencing "nothingness", and the importance of direct realization over intellectual assent.
- The philosophers' ideas are interconnected, with concepts like existential guilt, the will to nothingness, and the horror of a vacuum being discussed, and the authors' critiques of naturalism and conventional approaches to religion are also highlighted, with some arguing that true faith and understanding can only be achieved through a personal and direct experience of the transcendent.
- The text also touches on the idea that humans have a tendency to attach themselves to worldly concerns, as illustrated by Vivekananda's fishwife analogy, and that this attachment prevents them from recognizing or desiring higher truths, with the philosophers arguing that a confrontation with the "nothingness" and the transcendent is necessary to truly understand human existence.

Emptiness, the Self, and the Spiritual Journey

- The section critiques the concept of possessiveness in the "self-life" and explores the idea of "[[Śūnyatā | Emptiness]]" through the perspectives of various philosophers and spiritual leaders, including Suzuki, a Japanese Buddhist scholar who introduced Zen Buddhism to the West.
- The spiritual journey is described by Thomas Keating, an American Trappist monk, as a movement from "no one" to "everyone", while [[Plato]], an ancient Greek philosopher, discusses the soul's longing for beauty and the perception of those who turn away from worldly concerns as mad.
- The concept of emptiness and the [[Transcendence (philosophy) | transcendence]] of the material world are central to the teachings of Buddha, also known as [[The Buddha | Siddhartha Gautama]], the founder of Buddhism, and are also explored by Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, an anonymous Christian theologian and mystic, who describes God as beyond being and nothingness.
- Other philosophers and spiritual leaders, such as Meister Eckhart, [[Miguel de Molinos]], and [[Ernest Becker]], also contribute to the discussion, with Eckhart equating God with nothingness, Molinos emphasizing the importance of inner annihilation, and Becker posing the problem of reconciling saintliness with worldly action.
- The section also references the ideas of various other thinkers, including Terence McKenna, who viewed psychedelics as a means to access a "lost continent in the human mind" equated with God, and Thomas Ligotti, who references Zapffe's theory of consciousness as a tragic blunder and Meilinger's interpretation in light of Zen Buddhism.
- Additionally, the experiences and ideas of individuals such as the August 27th Experiencer, who had a profound psychedelic experience, and philosophers like [[Søren Kierkegaard]], [[Augustine of Hippo | St. Augustine]], [[William James]], [[Lev Shestov]], [[Blaise Pascal]], and [[Peter Wessel Zapffe]], are also mentioned, highlighting the complexities and nuances of the human condition and the nature of existence.
- The section also touches on the ideas of Socrates, who emphasized self-knowledge and questioning assumptions, and [[Seraphim Rose]], who interpreted anxiety and the "abyss" as the nothingness from which God calls humanity into being, as well as St. Theresa of Ávila, a Spanish Carmelite nun and mystic.

Existential Dread and the Abyss of Meaninglessness

- The concept of existential dread is described as a profound and unsettling feeling that arises from the confrontation with the fundamental meaninglessness of existence, the imminence of death, and the ultimate insignificance of the world and its concerns, as discussed by various philosophers and thinkers, including Miguel de Unamuno, Johan O. Tønnessen, Leo Tolstoy, and Sigmund Freud.
- Existential dread is intricately linked to the experience of [[Nihilism | nihilism]], which posits that life and the universe lack inherent meaning, value, or purpose, and is characterized by a deep-seated anxiety in the face of the "nothing" that can lead to a confrontation with the "bottomless abyssus humanae conscientiae", revealing the "nightmare of existence".
- The philosophy of [[Martin Heidegger]] is central to understanding existential dread, which he refers to as 'Angst', a specific kind of anxiety that discloses the "insignificance of the world" and reveals the "nullity of that with which one can concern oneself" and the impossibility of grounding one's being on worldly objects of concern.
- The sources suggest that this "original dread" is often suppressed or disguised in everyday life, as individuals tend to "fall" into the "everydayness" of the world, getting lost in the "they-self" and inauthentic being, which involves distractions, "idle talk", and adherence to public norms, and that many attempts to avoid or alleviate the underlying sense of nothingness and the potential for existential dread are discussed, including the ideas of Thomas à Kempis, Thomas Meister, and others.
- The concept of existential dread is also related to the ideas of other thinkers, such as [[Peter Wessel Zapffe]], who is mentioned in the context of existential dread, and [[Miguel de Molinos]], who is referenced as St. Molinos, and whose ideas are associated with the quote "In life, man proposes, God disposes", highlighting the complexity and depth of the concept of existential dread.

Existential Dread and the Search for Meaning

- The experience of existential dread is described as deeply discomforting and terrifying, and is often accompanied by a sense of [[Nihilism | nihilism]] and the realization of the fundamental lack of intrinsic meaning in life and the universe, which can lead to a confrontation with the ultimate insignificance of the world and its concerns, and the impossibility of finding meaning or purpose in a seemingly meaningless world.
- The text discusses the concept of existential dread, which is a profound confrontation with the meaninglessness of existence and the imminence of death, and how people often respond to it by fleeing into distracting activities, embracing superficial optimism, or clinging to traditional religious frameworks.
- Some sources argue that many forms of religion are actually cultural manifestations of the fear of nothingness, and that an "optimistic scientism" can be a form of "unthinking" that regards empirical reality as the ultimate truth without questioning the deeper reasons for existence.
- However, the experience of confronting the "Nothing of the world" can potentially open the way to "[[Transcendence (philosophy) | Transcendence]]" or an encounter with "the Other", which is not necessarily tied to traditional religious or supernatural concepts, but refers to something "Wholly-Other" that is not entirely deconstructive or naturalistic.
- The sources differentiate between a "worldly" or "diminished" experience of nihilism and a "Transcendent" or "augmented" version, which might involve mystical or psychedelic experiences that dissolve the ego and lead to a sense of unity, as discussed by philosophers such as Heidegger.
- A significant challenge highlighted is the incommunicable nature of the experience of existential dread and the difficulty of articulating it in language, which leads to a suggestion for the development of a "language of Nihilism" or "Nihiltheism" that could authentically engage with the concept of nothingness while also allowing for the possibility of transcendence.
- The awareness of death, or "Being-Towards-Death" as described by Heidegger, is presented as a key aspect of authentic existence and is intrinsically linked to existential dread, and confronting the finitude of one's own existence and the possibility of death can lead to a turning inward and an attempt to discover one's "True-Self".
- The text concludes that existential dread is a fundamental adjustment that reveals the insignificance of the world and is often suppressed by our engagement with inauthentic everydayness, but that it might hold the potential for a deeper understanding of existence and a possible encounter with something beyond the purely naturalistic.

Nothingness, Dread, and the Search for Meaning

- The concept of "Nothingness" is a central theme in human existence, and it is connected to profound experiences, as stated by philosophers such as Heidegger, who notes that "In dread, being as a whole becomes untenable," revealing the precariousness of existence and the underlying "no-thing."
- Human existence is uniquely positioned in its relationship to "Nothingness," and confronting or acknowledging this void is integral to understanding our being, as human existence can relate to being only if it is itself beholden to "no-thing."
- The "Why-Question" is seen as a "provocative happening" that can lead beyond superficial understandings of existence, and it is through this questioning that individuals can begin to understand the nature of "Nothingness" and its role in human existence.
- According to thinkers such as Cioran, humans largely avoid or suppress the confrontation with this fundamental "dread" and the underlying "no-thing" through various distractions and attachments to the "world," including banality and routine, and attachment to worldly concerns, which can be seen as a form of "sheer madness."
- The drive to find "worldly meaning" is questioned in the face of "deep time" and the eventual demise of the universe, and the insistence on personal meaning despite this cosmic backdrop can be seen as a "neurotic form of justification for the value of being alive," as noted by philosophers such as [[Plato]] and [[Swami Vivekananda | Vivekananda]].
- A confrontation with "Nothingness" can lead to experiences described as mystical, characterized by ego dissolution and a sense of unity, as noted by thinkers such as Suzuki, who describes Buddhist "[[Śūnyatā | Emptiness]]" as a "zero full of infinite possibilities, it is a void of inexhaustible contents."
- The mystical experience often involves the eradication of the ego, and a sense of encountering a "bigger something," or a transcendent reality beyond the individual self, as noted by philosophers such as Huxley, who states that "To the extent that there is attachment to 'I,' 'Me,' 'Mine,' there is no attachment to, and therefore no unitive knowledge of, the divine Ground."
- The path to this experience often involves renunciation and humility, and a willingness to let go of worldly desires and ambitions, as noted by Keating, who states that "To be no one is to be everyone, to be no self is to be the true Self, to be nothing is to be everything, in a sense, it is to be God."
- The ability to articulate or understand complex experiences is intricate and often involves unique forms of language and symbolism, as suggested by Buber, who views anxiety as a conduit to understanding, describing it as the language that stirs the creature between the realms of plantlike security and spiritual risk.
- The concept of the "eternal You" and the "It-world and It-speech" is distinguished by Buber, implying that direct experience transcends objectification and linguistic limitations, and the term "[[Transcendence (philosophy) | Transcendence]]" is proposed as a more neutral term to describe this realm, avoiding pre-conceived notions and supernatural connotations.
- Music, particularly Bach's organ music, is suggested as a powerful tool to guide one through the confrontation with "Nothingness" and the death of the "I", with Nietzsche highlighting music as "the language of the will itself, speaking straight out of the 'abyss'".

The Divided Self, Nihilism, and Transcendence

- Human experience is characterized by a tension between naturalistic inclinations and a potential for transcendence, with philosophers such as [[Søren Kierkegaard | Kierkegaard]], [[Augustine of Hippo | St. Augustine]], and [[Plato]] describing the internal conflict as a struggle between "two wills", one carnalnatural and the other spiritual.
- The naturalistic side of humanity constantly pulls individuals down into the world and its finite concerns, making it difficult to sustain philosophical or religious ideals, leading to a feeling of "being guilty", and Heidegger's concept of "Being-guilty" is presented as stemming from the "always falling" condition of being dragged down by pure naturalism.
- The experience of [[Nihilism | nihilism]] can be a necessary step towards a deeper understanding or a transcendent experience, as it necessitates giving up attachment to superficial meanings and illusions, and philosophers such as Nietzsche and Plato discuss the concept of "horror vacui" and the ascetic ideal, which suggests that humans prefer to will even nothingness rather than not will at all.
- Embracing nihilism or seeking [[Transcendence (philosophy) | transcendence]] can appear as madness from a worldly perspective, but can ultimately lead to a state of "no self" or "nothingness", which can be the "top", a point of true self-discovery and connection to the divine, as suggested by Keating, and Huxley's concept of Nihiltheism introduces the idea that the ultimate reality is not necessarily aligned with human moral frameworks.

Direct Experience, Nihilism, and the Search for Meaning

- The sources discussed in the document emphasize the personal and incommunicable nature of experiences related to nihilism and transcendence, highlighting the importance of individual research and personal connection with one's chosen ideal or deity, as advised by [[Swami Vivekananda | Vivekananda]].
- Vivekananda stresses that intellectual understanding of these concepts is insufficient, and true realization can only be achieved through direct experience, rather than mere intellectual assent, and also advises keeping one's personal connections and ideals sacred and secret.
- The sources raise several critical questions and challenges, including whether suicide is a rational option in the face of nothingness, whether a philosophy of religion can be built upon the concept of nothingness, and how humans can find meaning in a seemingly meaningless world, with questions from notable thinkers like Becker and Shestov, such as how one can be both a saint and engaged in the world, and how nothingness turned into something and gained power.

Nihilism, Dread, and the Encounter with Nothingness

- The concept of [[Nihilism | nihilism]] is presented as a profound encounter with the fundamental nothingness underlying existence, which can be a catalyst for ego dissolution and potentially transcendent or mystical states, and is often marked by dread and a rejection of superficial worldly meaning, leading to a confrontation with the "Other" and a realization of a deeper reality beyond the limitations of language and conventional understanding.
- Existential dread is understood as a profound and unsettling feeling that arises from the confrontation with the fundamental meaninglessness of existence, the imminence of death, and the ultimate insignificance of the world and its concerns, and is intricately linked to the experience of nihilism, as described by philosophers like [[Martin Heidegger]], who is central to understanding existential dread in these sources.
- The concept of "Angst" or existential dread, as referred to by Heidegger, is a specific kind of anxiety that reveals the insignificance of the world and the impossibility of grounding one's being on worldly objects of concern, leading to a feeling of "being as a whole becomes untenable".
- In the experience of dread, the familiar entities of the world no longer offer any involvement or meaning, and this is not simply the absence of things but a positive revelation of "no-thing", which is a fundamental aspect of human existence that is often suppressed or disguised in everyday life.
- According to the sources, people tend to "fall" into the "everydayness" of the world, getting lost in the "they-self" and inauthentic being, which involves distractions, "idle talk", and adherence to public norms, serving as a flight from the "not-at-home" feeling and the uncanniness that lies within human existence.
- Various attempts to avoid or alleviate the underlying sense of nothingness and the potential for existential dread are discussed, including fleeing into activities of distraction, embracing a superficial "optimism", and clinging to traditional religious frameworks, which are sometimes seen as "cultural manifestations of the fear of nothingness".
- However, the sources also suggest that confronting the "Nothing of the world" can potentially open the way to "[[Transcendence (philosophy) | Transcendence]]" or an encounter with "the Other", which is not necessarily tied to traditional religious or supernatural concepts, but rather refers to something "Wholly-Other" that is not entirely deconstructive or naturalistic.
- The experience of existential dread is challenging to articulate in language, and existing languages and philosophies are questioned for their adequacy in capturing the essence of this experience, leading to a suggestion for the development of a "language of [[Nihilism]]" or "Nihiltheism" that could authentically engage with the concept of nothingness.
- The awareness of death, or "Being-Towards-Death" as described by Heidegger, is presented as a key aspect of authentic existence and is intrinsically linked to existential dread, and confronting the finitude of one's own existence and the possibility of death at any moment can lead to a turning inward and an attempt to discover one's "True-Self".

The Nihilistic Experience and the Dissolution of the Ego

- The experience of Nihilism is a profound and often terrifying encounter with the "Nothingness" of the world and the self, revealing the finite and transient nature of all worldly concerns and leading to a sense of dread and the untenability of one's previous understanding of being.
- This Nihilistic experience is not merely an intellectual conclusion about meaninglessness, but a fundamental experience that can be triggered by a breakdown of normal distractions or through altered states of consciousness, and it relates to everyday human existence as people constantly engage in "nihilating behavior" to suppress the underlying awareness of this void.
- The concept of "dread" plays a significant role in understanding [[Nihilism]], as it is the primary mode through which the "no-thing" or Nothingness originally discloses itself, and while often suppressed, dread's presence subtly permeates existence, revealing the precariousness of being and the underlying void.
- The sources connect the "Nihilistic experience" to concepts of self and ego, suggesting that the experience of Nihilism often involves an "eradication of the ego" or a "dissolution of the 'I'," leading to a disconnect from the physical body and a feeling of incorporeality, but also potentially revealing a "bigger something," a deeper sense of unity or pure consciousness that transcends the individual "I."
- The "Nihilistic experience" can be considered a "religious" or "transcendent" experience, as the sources argue for a "Transcendent Nihilism," suggesting that the experience of Nothingness can indeed have spiritual dimensions, and this [[Transcendence (philosophy) | transcendence]] is not necessarily about supernatural entities but rather about an encounter with the "Wholly-Other," a feature of human experience that goes beyond the purely deconstructive or naturalistic.
- Practices like renunciation, the use of psychedelics, and the experience of music can relate to confronting Nihilism and the possibility of transcendence, as renunciation is seen as a necessary preparation for experiencing the "Other" beyond the Nothing of the world, and these practices can help individuals confront and transcend the Nihilistic experience, potentially leading to a deeper understanding of existence and a sense of unity or pure consciousness.

Psychedelics, Music, and the Divided Self

- The concept of psychedelic experiences is described as having the ability to dissolve the ego, facilitate a confrontation with Nothingness, and potentially lead to mystical experiences, with music being a unique language that can guide one through this confrontation and aid in the "death of the 'I'".
- The "divided-self" is a fundamental aspect of the human condition, referring to the inherent tension within human consciousness between naturalistic, worldly inclinations and the capacity for transcendence or awareness of Nothingness, leading to feelings of frustration, guilt, and the inability to consistently live up to philosophical or religious ideals.
- Engaging with the Nihilistic experience can lead to profound insights and a potential "transfiguration" by confronting the Nothingness of the world and the self, resulting in a "death" of the ego and access to a deeper level of reality or unity, but it is cautioned that this experience should not be simplistically embraced.

Dread, Mystical Experience, and the Divided Self

- According to Heidegger, the experience of dread reveals "no-thing", which is mostly suppressed in daily life, but remains as a latent tremor, and human existence can only relate to "being" if it is fundamentally beholden to "no-thing", suggesting that the capacity to transcend mere being is essential to what it means to exist as human.
- The mystical experience is described as the eradication of the worldly ego or "I", leading to a disconnect from the physical body and a sense of incorporeality, where the ego dissolves, and a different sort of "you" remains and encounters a "bigger something", suggesting a meeting with something beyond the individual self.
- Buddhist [[Śūnyatā | Emptiness]], as described by Suzuki, is not absence, extinction, or unoccupancy, but rather Absolute Emptiness, transcending dualities like subjectobject and birthdeath, and is described as a zero full of infinite possibilities, a void of inexhaustible contents that makes all things possible.
- Renunciation, specifically the dissolution of the ego and worldly desires, plays a necessary role in experiencing the "Other", as it allows for a purer state of humility that enables a clearer encounter with the Transcendent, and the "divided-self" refers to the internal conflict between the naturalistic and transcendent aspects of human consciousness, often described as two opposing wills.
- The concept of [[Nihilism | nihilism]] is explored in the text, where it is described as the experience and recognition of the fundamental meaninglessness and valuelessness of worldly existence and human endeavors, which can lead to a feeling of guilt and the inability to consistently live up to ideals.
- Nietzsche's concept of "Absurd Valuation" is discussed, where he describes it as the moment when one experiences great contempt for their own happiness, reason, and virtues, recognizing them as poverty, dirt, and wretched contentment that fail to justify existence itself.
- The "multi-verse" theory is mentioned, which suggests that an infinite number of universes and "you's" exist, leading to the conclusion that subjective morality and meaning are ultimately empty, as every possible choice will be made by one of these "you's", rendering any individual decision seemingly trivial and illusory.
- The text also explores the concept of "horror vacui", or the horror of a vacuum or emptiness, which Nietzsche relates to the ascetic ideal, stating that humans would rather will nothingness than not will at all, indicating a deep-seated need for a goal or purpose.

Music, the Divided Self, and Altered States of Consciousness

- Bach's music is presented as a guide through the confrontation with the "Other" and the death of the ego, offering a form of holistic order and expressing meditations on death, which can be seen as a way to cope with the experience of [[Nihilism | nihilism]].
- The concept of the "divided-self" is analyzed, where the struggle between the "naturalistic" and "transcendent" aspects of human existence leads to a division, resulting in a feeling of guilt and the inability to consistently live up to ideals, and potential paths towards resolving or understanding this division are discussed.
- The role of altered states of consciousness, particularly the "psychedelic experience", in facilitating mystical experiences and the dissolution of the ego is examined, and its relationship to the confrontation with nihilism is considered.
- The text also compares and contrasts different understandings of [[Śūnyatā | emptiness]], including Heidegger's "no-thing" and Suzuki's concept of Buddhist Emptiness, and how these relate to the broader themes of nihilism and [[Transcendence (philosophy) | transcendence]].
- Finally, the critique of conventional approaches to life and the pursuit of happiness is examined, and the authors' suggestions for engaging with the fundamental questions of existence in the face of nihilistic insights are discussed, including the adoption of the term "Transcendence" and the exploration of the relationship between the experience of "Nothingness" and the possibility of a "religious" or "Transcendent" interpretation.

Mystical Experience, the Ego, and the Other

- The concept of mystical experience is characterized by the eradication of the ego, a sense of unity or interconnectedness, and often a feeling of encountering something beyond the ordinary self and world, which is referred to as the "Other".
- The ego, or the worldly "I" or sense of self, is often associated with individual identity, desires, and attachments to the material world, and in mystical experiences, it is described as dissolving or being transcended through the process of renunciation.
- Renunciation is the act of giving up or letting go of worldly desires, attachments, and the illusions of the ego as a necessary step towards experiencing something beyond the self, which can lead to a deeper understanding of the human condition and the concept of the "Divided-Self".
- The "Divided-Self" refers to the internal conflict within human consciousness between the naturalistic, world-bound aspects and the transcendent, spiritual longings, which can lead to feelings of guilt and frustration, and is related to the concept of "Absurd Valuation" described by Nietzsche.
- Nietzsche's concept of "Absurd Valuation" describes the point at which one's own deeply held values, happiness, reason, and virtues become repulsive, revealing their ultimate inadequacy in justifying existence, and is connected to the idea of "Horror Vacui", or the fear of the vacuum, which is the human will's aversion to [[Śūnyatā | emptiness]].
- The term "Other" is used to describe something beyond the individual self and the natural world, often encountered in mystical or transcendent experiences, and is intentionally vague, avoiding specific religious connotations, and is distinct from the concept of "Naturalism", which is the worldview that the physical universe is the only reality.

The Language of Nihilism and its Challenges

- The discussion of the language of [[Nihilism | nihilism]] revolves around the inherent difficulties in articulating an experience centered on "Nothingness" and the potential for developing a unique linguistic framework to navigate and understand this concept, which is related to the idea of "Nihiltheism", the idea that ultimate reality is not moral and that union with this reality leads to a non-moral state.
- The development of a common language to describe the experience of nihilism is exceptionally difficult due to its non-transferable nature, as highlighted by philosophers such as Nietzsche and [[Augustine of Hippo | St. Augustine]], who have grappled with the challenges of articulating the concept of nothingness and the human encounter with it.
- The concept of nihilism is explored as an experience that resists linguistic capture, with [[Thérèse of Lisieux | Therese of Lisieux]] noting that one must pass through the "dark tunnel" to understand its essence, which cannot be fully grasped through intellectual understanding alone.
- The sources suggest the need for a "new language" of nihilism, one that moves beyond traditional religious or naturalist worldviews and is instead symbolic, self-critical, and open to reinterpretation, with the term "Nihiltheism" proposed as a grounding word for this genre of human experience.
- Martin Heidegger's work, particularly in "[[Being and Time]]", is presented as a comprehensive groundwork for developing a language of nihilism, with his concepts such as anxiety, authenticity, and Being-Towards-Death capturing key insights into the human condition that allow for an interpretation of the nihilistic experience.
- A language of nihilism is envisioned as a means to confront the Nothingness directly, rather than escaping it, and to potentially reveal a form of [[Transcendence (philosophy) | Transcendence]] experienced through it, with the goal of facilitating a "face to face" encounter with the "nightmare of existence".
- This proposed language is tentative, replete with doubt, and constantly asking "Why?", rather than offering confirmations, and is envisioned as a "map" through the Nothing of the world, potentially leading towards Transcendence.
- The development of this language is intrinsically linked to the direct experience of [[Nihilism | nihilism]], with intellectual notions alone considered "stillborn", and the experience of Nothingness is acknowledged as rare, but potentially a groundwork for Transcendence.

Augmented Nihilism and the Critique of Religious Language

- The concept of "Augmented Nihilism" is associated with mystical experiences, representing a true "opening up" of the Transcendent, and attempts to reconcile the apparent conflict between naturalism and transcendence, viewing them as interconnected aspects of a wider nihilistic experience.
- The need for a language of nihilism also stems from a critique of traditional religious language, which is seen as a "cultural manifestation of the fear of nothingness", and the proposed language aims to reconcile disparities between seemingly opposed viewpoints, such as Heidegger's "atheism" and Thomas Merton's "theism".
- The act of attempting to develop a language for a fundamentally non-transferable experience creates inherent challenges and paradoxes, and the sources grapple with questions of whether such a language could have altered the trajectory of individuals who have lived out nihilism to the point of despair.

A New Linguistic Framework for Nihilism and Transcendence

- The development of a new linguistic framework is proposed, one that is grounded in direct experience, particularly the confrontation with the Nothingness of existence, and draws on thinkers like [[Martin Heidegger]] to explore the potential for [[Transcendence (philosophy) | Transcendence]] revealed through [[Nihilism | nihilism]].
- This new language is envisioned as tentative, symbolic, and continuously questioning, aiming to map the complex terrain between the experience of meaninglessness and the elusive possibility of something "more", and is not intended to be literal or complete but rather open to reinterpretation and self-criticism.
- The sources discussed, including the works of Heidegger, St. [[Thérèse of Lisieux | Therese of Lisieux]], and Cioran, highlight the profound challenges in articulating the experience of nihilistic "nothingness" and the potential for a unique "language of nihilism", which would necessarily embody a symbolic nature.
- Heidegger's philosophy, particularly his concept of "Being-Towards-Death" and the analysis of human existence in the face of the "nothing" of the world, is presented as a potential foundation for this language, which should aim to bring one "face to face" with the "nightmare of existence".
- The proposed "new language" of nihilism recognizes that finite human language is necessarily symbolic in nature when speaking of the Other, and suggests the term "Nihiltheism" as a potential grounding for this "genre" of human experience, which should account for the possibility of Transcendence as revealed through nihilism without adhering strictly to religious or naturalist worldviews.
- The concept of "aﬃrmative substance" of Nothing is also explored, with Cioran suggesting that the initial revelation of any monastery is that "everything is nothing", thus beginning all mysticisms, and highlighting the importance of developing a language that can express the complexity and depth of human existential experience.

Symbolic Resonance Nothing and the Language of Nihilism

- The concept of "symbolic resonance nothing" refers to the idea that while the core experience of nihilistic nothingness may be difficult or impossible to articulate directly, a symbolic language can be developed to resonate with this experience, explore its implications, and potentially point towards a realm beyond it.
- The experience of nihilistic nothingness is considered to be a profound and complex phenomenon that can be challenging to express through existing language, with thinkers like St. [[Thérèse of Lisieux | Therese of Lisieux]] and Cioran highlighting the non-transferable nature of such deep existential experiences.
- Traditional religious languages and purely naturalistic perspectives are criticized for often providing pre-packaged "answers" and "rational defenses" that close off the mystery of existence, rather than opening it up, and a new language of [[Nihilism | nihilism]] is envisioned as something beyond mere naturalistic descriptions or the "dead 'solutions' of the worldly philosophies and religions".
- The language of nihilism should be characterized by its self-critical nature, its openness to reinterpretation, and its focus on confronting the "terror of existence" rather than offering easy solutions or distractions, with the goal of bringing one "face to face" with the "nightmare of existence".
- Martin Heidegger's philosophy is presented as a potential foundation for developing a language of nihilism, which would aim to navigate between the limitations of traditional religious languages and purely naturalistic perspectives, and provide a framework for exploring the implications of nihilistic nothingness.
- The concept of "Nihiltheism" is mentioned as a potential framework for this symbolic language of nothingness, which could provide a unique perspective on the relationship between nihilism and the possibility of [[Transcendence (philosophy) | transcendence]], referred to as "the Other" or Transcendence.
- The concept of "Being-Towards-Death" by Heidegger is seen as a framework for understanding the nihilistic experience, where anxiety arises from the "nothing" of the world, and the world's insignificance is disclosed in dread, rather than being just an absence.
- The proposed "new language" of nihilism, potentially referred to as "Nihiltheism", aims to account for the possibility of Transcendence within human experience, without adhering to traditional religious or naturalist worldviews, and should be symbolic, open to reinterpretation, and self-critical.
- This language recognizes the limitations of finite human language in speaking of the Other, and suggests that the experience of nihilistic nothingness may not be the final word, but could potentially open a path towards something beyond, as indicated by Cioran's perspective that "everything is nothing" and that God is the positive expression of nothingness.

The Symbolic Language of Nihilism and Transcendence

- Traditional religious languages and purely naturalistic perspectives are criticized for providing pre-packaged answers and failing to grasp the full depth of the nihilistic experience, and the "language of [[Nihilism]]" seeks to navigate between these limitations, focusing on confronting the "terror of existence" rather than offering easy solutions or distractions.
- The concept of "symbolic resonance nothing" refers to the idea that a symbolic language can be developed to resonate with the experience of nihilistic nothingness, explore its implications, and potentially point towards a realm beyond it, characterized by its self-critical nature, openness to reinterpretation, and focus on confronting the "terror of existence".

Nihilism, Transcendence, and the Need for a Symbolic Language

- The exploration of nihilism considers perspectives from philosophers, mystics, and scientific viewpoints, questioning traditional approaches and hinting at practices like solitude, music, and altered states of consciousness as potential avenues for confronting the fundamental reality of meaninglessness and the possibility of something beyond.
- The development of a symbolic language of Nihilism is considered crucial for navigating the complexities of the nihilistic experience, which encompasses both the "Nothing of the world" and a potential "Transcendental aspect of meaninglessness", as argued by various sources, including references to the works of philosophers such as Heidegger.
- Traditional languages, whether religious or purely naturalistic, are deemed inadequate for fully capturing the nuances of this experience, with cliches and "idle talk" failing to convey the depth of [[Nihilism]], and even established frameworks like Christianity struggling to articulate profound encounters with nothingness.
- The proposed language of Nihilism is explicitly described as symbolic rather than literal, acknowledging the limitations of language to fully grasp the Infinite or the "Wholly-Other", and is not intended to provide definitive answers or a "complete" understanding in a literal sense, but rather to act as a "map" through the "Nothing of the world".
- The concept of Nihiltheism is introduced as a potential grounding for this symbolic language, which seeks to provide an adequate account of human experience that allows for the possibility of [[Transcendence (philosophy) | Transcendence]] as revealed through Nihilism, and is characterized by being self-critical, open to re-interpretation, and embracing doubt.
- The language of Nihiltheism is influenced by the ideas of philosophers such as Heidegger, who in his work "[[Being and Time]]" developed a comprehensive language about the human condition, and is also compared to the perspective of [[Thomas Merton]], highlighting the possibility of reconciling atheistic and theistic viewpoints through a language that focuses on the shared experience of confronting fundamental nothingness.
- The need for a symbolic language also stems from the idea that Transcendence is not something to be grasped intellectually or through dogma, but rather experienced directly, as emphasized by quotes from authors such as Cioran and Huxley, who suggest that the ultimate Reality is not clearly apprehended by ordinary people and that a language of Nihilism and Transcendence might only resonate with those who have genuinely confronted the depths of meaninglessness.
- The concept of the "Other" remains somewhat obscure, but points towards an aspect of this developing language that transcends easy definition, and the overall goal of the language of Nihilism is to bring one "face to face" with the "nightmare of existence" and to facilitate a "pushing through the Nothingness" into its "Transcendental forms", as a means of exploring something that is "not wholly natural" yet still experienced and discussed within the world.

Nihiltheism and the Exploration of Nothingness

- The language discussed in the text aims to explore the experience of Nothingness and the possibility of encountering something "Wholly-Other" without relying on pre-packaged religious or naturalistic explanations, and it utilizes the concept of Nihiltheism as a framework for this endeavor, grounding the exploration of [[Transcendence (philosophy) | Transcendence]] within the unique context of the nihilistic experience itself.
- The text references the ideas of thinkers like Heidegger, Merton, and others, who contribute to the understanding of [[Nihilism | nihilism]] from various angles, including its relationship to language, mysticism, naturalism, nonexistence, and philosophy, and it suggests that their perspectives can illuminate a potential shared language for understanding the nihilistic human experience.
- Heidegger's philosophy, particularly as presented in [[Being and Time]], is positioned as foundational for developing a language of Nihilism, providing concepts like anxiety, moments of vision, everydayness, the call of conscience, authenticity, uncanniness, and Being-Towards-Death, which can capture key insights into the human experience of Nihilism on non-naturalistic terms while still being grounded in the naturalistic aspect of human existence.
- Heidegger's concept of anxiety, particularly in the face of Nothingness, is a central element in understanding the core of nihilistic experience, and it is characterized as a fundamental unease stemming from the awareness of existence itself and its potential for non-being, rather than a worldly fear of a specific object.
- The text also highlights the potential for a shared vocabulary to articulate the nihilistic human experience, potentially even hinting at transcendence, and it invites exploration of the specific symbolic terms or concepts that this language might employ, drawing from the ideas of thinkers like Heidegger and Merton.

Anxiety, Being-Towards-Death, and Authenticity

- The concept of anxiety is described by Buber as "the stirring of the creature between the realms of plantlike security and spiritual risk", which further illuminates the precarious state of human existence, and this idea is connected to Heidegger's concept of "Being-Towards-Death", which describes human existence as fundamentally oriented towards death, shaping our understanding of being and time.
- Heidegger's philosophy distinguishes between authentic existence, which involves facing one's finitude and the groundlessness of existence, and inauthentic existence, which involves fleeing into the "everydayness" of the world and the "They-self", and this distinction is relevant to the nihilistic experience, which can shatter the illusions of inauthentic existence and push one towards a more authentic engagement with the void.

Heidegger's Critique of Traditional Frameworks and Merton's Theistic Perspective

- Heidegger critiques traditional metaphysics and theology, avoiding terms like "atheistic" or "theistic" as he sees them as restrictive for a full analysis of the human situation, and instead emphasizes the importance of relentless questioning and opening up the mystery of Being, rather than settling on pre-determined answers.
- [[Thomas Merton]], a Trappist monk, approaches the experience of nothingness from a distinctly religious perspective, yet his ideas converge with Heidegger's, as he speaks of the "bankruptcy of a constant outward-seeking, hectic search for 'meaning' within life" and the importance of penetrating into the darkness and nothingness with awe and reverence.
- Merton's perspective suggests that the experience of inner [[Śūnyatā | emptiness]] can be a space for encountering the divine, and his emphasis on reverence for mystery aligns with Heidegger's emphasis on opening up the mystery of Being through questioning, and this shared emphasis on the importance of confronting fundamental nothingness could potentially reconcile the superficial disparities between Heidegger's "atheism" and Merton's "theism".
- The potential shared language for understanding nihilistic human experience emerges from the recognition of the primacy of the experience of nothingness itself, regardless of the theistic or atheistic interpretations that might follow, and both Heidegger and Merton point to this fundamental encounter with the void or inner emptiness as a crucial aspect of the human condition.
- Both perspectives critique a constant immersion in worldly affairs as a way of avoiding this fundamental encounter, and instead suggest a posture of open inquiry in the face of the unknown, with Heidegger's relentless "Why?" and Merton's reverence for mystery both emphasizing the importance of questioning and seeking to understand the mystery of Being.

A Shared Language for Nihilistic Human Experience

- The concept of a shared language for nihilistic human experience is explored, emphasizing the importance of a questioning stance and the need to avoid premature closure through dogma or simplistic answers, as highlighted by thinkers such as Heidegger and Merton.
- The idea of the 'Wholly-Other' and [[Transcendence (philosophy) | Transcendence]] is discussed, with Heidegger using the term 'Other' in an intentionally obscure way, and Merton speaking of God as a transformative force within nothingness, highlighting the human yearning for something beyond the purely naturalistic.
- The use of symbolic language is suggested as a means to articulate profound experiences, as finite human language struggles to grasp the Infinite or the Wholly-Other, with both Heidegger and Merton using symbolic language to point towards realities that transcend direct conceptualization.
- A shared language for nihilistic human experience would likely focus on the direct experience of the void, a posture of relentless questioning and reverence for mystery, the inherent human yearning for transcendence, and the necessary use of symbolic language to articulate these profound and often ineffable encounters.
- The exploration of disparate perspectives, including those of Heidegger, Merton, Nietzsche, and Cioran, can help to reveal the common core of the human confrontation with the abyss and the potential for something more, and can provide a deeper understanding of the distinctions between naturalistic and transcendent interpretations of nihilistic experiences.

Naturalistic and Transcendent Interpretations of Nihilistic Experiences

- The discussion of nihilistic experiences highlights the fundamental encounter with the "Nothing" of the world, often leading to a confrontation with the apparent meaninglessness of existence, and the subsequent consequences that diverge significantly along naturalistic and transcendent lines, as presented in the sources.
- The naturalistic interpretation of nihilistic experiences views the encounter with "Nothing" through the lens of science, psychology, and a materialistic understanding of reality, where the meaninglessness is seen as an objective truth about the universe, devoid of any inherent purpose or value beyond what humans construct subjectively.
- This perspective suggests that the experience of confronting nothingness can be understood as a breakdown of normal psychological defenses, potentially leading to existential frustration, ontological despair, or sub-clinical depression, and that some individuals, like Mitchell Heisman, may even contemplate rational self-destruction due to their inability to find a language of [[Transcendence (philosophy) | transcendence]] within their nihilistic framework.
- In contrast, a transcendent interpretation posits that the encounter with the "Nothing" can be a gateway to something beyond the merely material, to "the Other," or to a deeper, often mystical, reality, where the experience of the "Nothing of the world" can lay the groundwork for a transcendent experience, an "opening up" to something beyond the mundane.
- The transcendent interpretation also suggests that mystical and psychedelic experiences, such as those facilitated by [[Psilocybin | psilocybin]], can easily dissolve the ego and facilitate a confrontation with the Infinite or "the Other," leading to a sense of unity and pure consciousness, and that renunciation of worldly distractions, ego, and self is often necessary to prepare for a clearer experience of "the Other".
- The consequences of a naturalistic [[Nihilism | nihilism]] can include despair and suicide, as seen in the case of Mitchell Heisman, while the consequences of a transcendent interpretation can include a sense of unity and transformation, leading to a radically altered perception of reality, as experienced by figures like St. John of the Cross, who underwent a profound transformation after confronting the transcendent aspect of nihilism.
- Ultimately, the naturalistic and transcendent interpretations of nihilistic experiences represent two distinct perspectives on the human encounter with "Nothing", with the naturalistic view often leading to a retreat into distraction and a avoidance of deeper philosophical questions, and the transcendent view offering a potential gateway to a deeper, more meaningful understanding of reality.

Nihilism, Transcendence, and the Search for True-Self

- The experiences of darkness and nothingness within a religious framework, as described by [[Thérèse of Lisieux | Therese of Lisieux]] and [[Thomas Merton]], can ultimately lead to a deeper understanding of the divine, highlighting the complex and multifaceted nature of nihilistic experiences.
- The concept of the "True-Self" and "the Other" is crucial in understanding the nihilistic experience, as it prompts a search for a true self that transcends naturalistic interpretations and avoids traditional religious myth, with "the Other" remaining a largely obscure but essential aspect of a transcendent interpretation.
- Various thinkers and mystics, including Heisman, Heidegger, St. Therese, Merton, Cioran, and Huxley, offer distinct perspectives on the nihilistic experience, with some, like Heisman, expressing a desire for a new language of [[Nihilism | nihilism]] that moves beyond naturalistic views, while others, like Heidegger, provide a philosophical framework for understanding the encounter with nothingness without resorting to traditional frameworks.
- Mystics like St. Therese and Merton describe profound experiences of darkness and nothingness within their religious frameworks, which resonate with the idea of [[Transcendence (philosophy) | transcendence]] within the nihilistic experience, although the use of religious terminology raises questions about the distinction between their experiences and more atheistic interpretations of nihilism.
- The challenge of language is a recurring theme, as existing language may be inadequate to capture the essence of the nihilistic experience, particularly its transcendent aspects, and the development of a "language of Nihilism" is proposed as a way to address this challenge, with the goal of confronting the transcendental yearning authentically without resorting to easy replacements or pre-packaged answers.
- The consequences of nihilistic experiences are significant, with purely naturalistic encounters potentially leading to despair and suicide, while transcendent interpretations suggest a possibility of moving beyond this despair, highlighting the importance of developing a nuanced understanding of the nihilistic experience and its various interpretations.

Faith, Suicide, and the Divergence in Interpreting Nihilism

- The concept of "faith" mentioned by Underhill refers to a fundamental experience of something beyond the "Nothing of the world", which may not necessarily be a traditional religious faith, but rather a means of navigating the nihilistic experience without succumbing to despair.
- The relationship between nihilistic experience and suicide is complex, and sources acknowledge that the suicide of someone who has confronted the "Nothing" may differ fundamentally from a "worldly suicide" driven by egoic loss, as highlighted in references 68-70.
